# writing / dialogue-only

**Prompt:** Write a scene of 250-350 words consisting entirely of dialogue between two characters.

Constraints:
- No narration, no action lines, no dialogue tags at all — not even "she said". Only the spoken lines, one per paragraph.
- The two speakers must be immediately distinguishable by voice alone: vocabulary, rhythm, sentence length, what they refuse to say.
- The reader must be able to work out, without being told: where they are, what their relationship is, and what happened shortly before the scene starts.
- One character is trying to avoid a subject; the other keeps returning to it.
- End on a line that reframes something said earlier.

## Repeat 1 (seed 0)

- Score: 9.0/10 — Dialogue shows clear avoidance pattern with reframe at end, setting implied through kitchen cues, and distinct voices.
- Tokens/s: 130.4
- Prefill tok/s: 2277.2
- TTFT: 0.13s
- Gen Time: 1003.53s
- Output Tokens: 130874
- **truncated** — stopped at the context limit, not by choice
- **opens with planning prose** — checks are measuring the reasoning

Draft 1:

"You're still in your shoes."
"Am I?"
"You've been standing in the kitchen for ten minutes staring at the fridge like it's going to tell you something."
"I was thinking about the tomatoes."
"The tomato plant. Right. It's in the garage now."
"It doesn't do much in the garage. But it's warm."
"It's a plant, David. It's not a person you can just hide in a garage from."
"Don't."
"Don't what? Don't say it out loud. It's already in the house. It's been in the house since Tuesday."
"You don't know anything about Tuesday."
"I know you came home at three in the morning and showered until the water ran cold, and I know you've slept with the radio on every night since, and I know you think I can't hear it picking up in the other room."
"I didn't think you'd pretend to be asleep."
"I've been very good at pretending. That's the whole trick, isn't it."
"It's not what you think."
"It's exactly what I think. You're leaving. I can see your bags by the stairs. Two of them. Which one is yours and which one is yours to take?"
"One bag is mine."
"So it's decided."
"I never said it was decided."
"You said the tomatoes need warmth. That's a person saying goodbye, David. That's the whole sentence. It's a goodbye."
"...I watered them the last three weeks."
"You watered them."
"Someone had to. I wasn't going to leave them dying."
"So you could water them, but you couldn't stay."
"I didn't say that."
"No. You never do. That's why I'm the one asking."
